  Why simplifying looks safe: cleanup.py finds your resources by their metadata,
  {"workshop": "ma201", "owner": <handle>}, not by name. The handle in the name
  only helps you tell environments apart in the Console when several people
  share one key.

  What the note suggests:
  - Use a plain name such as "ma201-vetter", or keep the handle and say why in
    the docstring.
  - If you change it, also change the agent name (ma201-vetter-{handle}) so the
    two match.
  - Rerun check.py --solution afterwards.

  I can make the change now, or keep adding notes while you dogfood.

❯ do we really need the metadata for the environment in step 1? the linked docs 
  don't mention it. what's the benefit?                                         

  Listed 1 directory, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ You don't need it there. Step 1 works without metadata, and check.py never
  checks it on the environment. Its only consumer is cleanup.py, which lists
  every environment on the key and deletes the ones tagged {"workshop": "ma201",
  "owner": <handle>}.

  What it buys you:
  - On a shared key, cleanup can't touch anyone else's environment.
  - If the local .ma201-state.json is lost (fresh clone, deleted file), cleanup
    can still find your environments. That's the only real advantage. Cleanup
    already deletes the uploaded file using just the id saved in state, and it
    could do the same for the environment.

  Where metadata does earn its place: sessions in stub 4. Stub 6
  (find_open_session) filters on metadata["owner"] to reattach to your session
  rather than a teammate's. That's a real use the learner sees pay off, so
  that's where to teach it.

  My recommendation: drop metadata from stub 1, and probably stub 3 (agent) too.
  Introduce it once, in stub 4, with stub 6 as the reason. cleanup.py would
  switch to the ids in the state file for the environment and agent, as it
  already does for the file. That's one fewer thing to copy blindly in the first
  five minutes, and it matches the docs you're linking. Should I add that to
  the dogfood notes, or make the change across agent.py, solutions/agent.py and
  cleanup.py and rerun the checks?

  Two things in your step 1 code, since you're dogfooding (hints only, per
  lab/AGENTS.md):
  - Packages: look at what ENV_PACKAGES already contains in provided.py:30, then
    at what you're passing as "packages".
  - Return value: the docstring says to return the environment's id. Check what
    environments.create(...) actually returns.

⏺ I think a skeleton with the values blanked out is the right fix. Your fumbling
  was about structure: the nesting inside config, the state.sync(..., lambda: 
  ...) wrapper, and the trailing .id. Those are lab plumbing, not Managed Agents
  concepts, so there's no learning lost by giving them away. The values are the
  part worth looking up in the docs.

  I'd blank the values selectively:
  - Keep visible: state.sync("environment_id", lambda: ...), .create(, the
    config={...} nesting, and .id. That's the plumbing.
  - Blank: the "type" value and the networking value. They're the concept, the
    docs link answers them, and blanking networking sets up the later switch to
    limited.
  - Show ENV_PACKAGES filled in. It's a provided constant, not a concept, and
    blanking it is how you got the double-wrapped dict.

  That gives you something like:

  return state.sync("environment_id", lambda: client.beta.environments.create(
      name=...,
      config={
          "type": ...,
          "networking": {...},
          "packages": ENV_PACKAGES,
      },
  ).id)

  There's no metadata line, assuming you go with the earlier note to drop it
  from stub 1.

  One thing to decide: if only stub 1 gets a skeleton, it's deliberate
  scaffolding that fades. Stubs 2–4 have less help and the ★ stubs have none.
  I'd keep it that way rather than adding skeletons to every stub, which would
  push the lab toward fill-in-the-blanks. It may be worth a sentence in
  DESIGN_NOTE.md.

  Should I fold this into the stub 1 note in dogfood-notes.md?

⏺ I've updated process/dogfood-notes.md. The stub 1 note now includes the
  blanked skeleton, with metadata=... kept in. The "drop metadata" note is now
  "keep metadata," with an optional docstring line on why it's there (cleanup on
  a shared API key).

  On combining steps 2 and 4: you're right that the order feels odd, but I
  wouldn't merge them fully. The session is created on every vet, while the
  upload should happen once. If the upload moved inside start_vet, it would
  either re-upload the tarball on every run or need its own state.sync call.
  Either way it blurs the "create once vs. once per vet" split, which is one of
  the lab's quieter lessons.

  I logged three options in the note:
  - (a) Reorder to environment → agent → file → session. The upload then sits
    right before the mount, matching the flow of the docs example. All the
    pieces stay as they are.
  - (b) Merge the upload into start_vet. This matches the docs example, but has
    the drawback above.
  - (c) Move upload_vendor into provided.py. Stub 4 then teaches only the mount
    (resources=[...]). The README already treats Files as a supporting concept,
    and learners would have one stub fewer to write.

  I'd go with (a). It fixes the ordering without losing anything, and it's
  mostly renumbering in server.py, check.py, README.md and both agent.py files.
  Choose (c) if you'd rather cut the lab's length. I haven't changed any code
  yet.

❯ when creating the agent - I don't like that claude code and the agent.py asks

⏺ I recommend making agent.py self-contained, so a learner never needs to open
  provided.py to finish a stub. provided.py would stay in the repo for
  debugging, but nothing would point learners at it.

  1. Add a "What you're given" block at the top of agent.py. This replaces line
  7 ("Everything you need from provided.py…") with one plain line per imported
  name, for example:
  - MODEL: the model id.
  - SYSTEM: the reviewer's system prompt.
  - TOOLS: [{"type": "agent_toolset_20260401"}, RECORD_VERDICT], meaning the
    built-in sandbox tools plus our one custom tool.
  - ENV_PACKAGES: {"type": "packages", "pip": ["pip-audit"]}, which you pass
    as-is.
  - answer_custom_tool(session_id, event): runs record_verdict locally and sends
    the result back.

  2. Show values inline only where the value is the concept. For stub 3, write
  out the TOOLS list. The agent_toolset_20260401 entry is the point of that
  stub: you never write a tool executor for bash. Long things like the full
  SYSTEM text and the RECORD_VERDICT schema get a one-phrase description, not a
  copy. That keeps the duplication small.

  3. Remove "in provided.py" from the stub hints (agent.py:74, 117, 149, 174).
  The function names are already imported, so naming the helper is enough. Do
  the same in README.md:72 ("Look at TOOLS in provided.py").

  4. Update lab/AGENTS.md.
  - Line 17: keep "reading provided.py" under things Claude can fully help with,
    since it's needed for debugging. Add a rule: when a participant asks what a
    constant or helper is, answer from the glossary at the top of agent.py or
    in a sentence, and don't send them to provided.py.
  - Line 34: reword "answer_custom_tool from provided.py" to "the imported
    helper answer_custom_tool".
  - Line 50: leave the provided.handle_tool mention. It's debugging context, not
    a pointer for learners.

  5. Guard against drift. Extend ground rule 4 in the root AGENTS.md ("keep
  stubs and solution in sync") to cover this: if a constant in provided.py
  changes, update the glossary at the top of agent.py. That's the cost of
  duplicating, and one sentence keeps it in check.

  Optional: renaming provided.py to _plumbing.py (or moving it into a plumbing/
  folder) would also signal "not for you." It touches the imports in agent.py,
  solutions/agent.py, server.py, check.py and cleanup.py, so it's more churn
  than it's worth unless dogfooding shows people still wander in.

  Should I add this to the dogfood notes as a finding?

❯ status - in another session I'm through stub 3 (                              
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
                                                                                
  1 ── ENVIRONMENT: the sandbox template every session is built from            
                                                                                
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
                                                                                
  def setup_environment() -> str:                                               
      """Create a cloud environment ONCE and return its id.                     
                                                                                
  - name:     f"ma201-{state.handle()}"                                         
  - config:   type "cloud", networking "unrestricted" (for now), and            
              packages=ENV_PACKAGES so pip-audit is pre-installed               
  - metadata: state.owner()                                                     
                                                                                
  Wrap the create call in state.sync("environment_id", lambda: ...) so that     
  re-running reuses the same environment instead of making a new one.           
                                                                                
  Docs: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/environments#create- 
  an-environment                                                                
                                                                                
  https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/environments#packages      
  """                                                                           
                                                                                
  return state.sync("environment_id", lambda: client.beta.environments.create(  
      name=f"ma201-{state.handle()}",                                           
      config={                                                                  
          "type": "cloud",                                                      
          "networking": {"type": "unrestricted"},                               
          "packages": ENV_PACKAGES                                              
      },                                                                        
      metadata = state.owner()                                                  
  ).id)                                                                         
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
                                                                                
  2 ── FILES: get the untrusted vendor code into Anthropic's storage            
                                                                                
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
                                                                                
  def upload_vendor() -> str:                                                   
      """Upload the vendor tarball ONCE (state key "file_id") and return the    
  file id.                                                                      
                                                                                
  vendor_tarball() gives you the bytes. The SDK accepts a                       
  (filename, bytes, mime_type) tuple: ("acme-csvkit.tar.gz", ...,               
  "application/gzip").                                                          
                                                                                
  Docs:                                                                         
  https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/files#uploading-files      
  """                                                                           
  return state.sync("file_id", lambda: client.files.upload(                     
      file=("acme-csvkit.tar.gz", vendor_tarball(), "application/gzip")         
      ).id)                                                                     
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
                                                                                
  3 ── AGENT: a versioned, server-side definition (model + prompt + tools)      
                                                                                
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── 
                                                                                
  def setup_agent() -> str:                                                     
      """Create the agent ONCE (state key "agent_id") and return its id.        
                                                                                
  Use MODEL, SYSTEM, TOOLS (the built-in toolset + our custom record_verdict    
  tool — have a look at provided.py), a name like                               
  f"ma201-vetter-{state.handle()}",                                             
  and metadata=state.owner().                                                   
                                                                                
  Notice what you are NOT writing: no agent loop, no tool executor for bash.    
                                                                                
  Docs: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/agent-setup#create-a 
  n-agent                                                                       
        https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/tools#custom-tools   
  """                                                                           
  return state.sync("agent_id", lambda: client.beta.agents.create(              
      name= "Steff's Dogfood Agent 1",                                          
      model=MODEL,                                                              
      system=SYSTEM,                                                            
      tools=TOOLS,                                                              
      metadata=state.owner()                                                    
  ).id)                                                                         
  ) but all the UI shows is stubs [Image #4] I wonder if it would be nice for   
  the learner to have like, a acronum bar or something at the top of the UI     
  that highlughts for the 4 concepts of managed angents ( agent, environment,   
  session, event) and highlights what's been done - maybe when they click or    
  hover over it it should pull from what was created too? before we add this to 
  dogfood notes, what do you think?                                             
  ⎿  [Image #4]

⏺ I like the idea. The real problem behind it is that stubs 1–3 create real
  server-side resources, but the UI shows only a checkmark and an id string. The
  thing a learner should take away is "these now exist on Anthropic's side, and
  here's what they hold," and the UI doesn't show that.

  I'd adjust it in two ways.

  1. Reshape the left column rather than adding a bar above it. That column
  already shows progress, so a bar on top would show the same progress twice.
  Instead, group the stubs under the four concepts:
  - Environment: setup_environment()
  - Agent: setup_agent()
  - Session: upload_vendor() (as the session's mounted resource), start_vet(),
    find_open_session()
  - Events: run_vet(), resume()

  The concept vocabulary then frames everything the learner does, without adding
  more UI. It also fits with the reorder you chose, which puts the file step
  right before the session. Files then reads as part of setting up the session
  rather than a fifth concept.

  2. Make each concept clickable, showing what the server actually holds.
  Clicking would call retrieve on the resource and show the useful fields:
  - Environment: networking, packages. This pays off again in stretch 8, when
    networking changes to limited.
  - Agent: version, model, tools. Seeing agent_toolset next to record_verdict
    makes the point of stub 3 concrete. It sets up stretch 9's versioning too.
  - Session: status, mounted resources, budget.
  - Events: lights up once the stream starts, perhaps with a running count.

  It reinforces the lesson "the server is the source of truth, not your
  process," which is what reattaching in stubs 6–7 depends on.

  Cost: a small read-only endpoint in server.py plus some UI work in static/.
  That's all plumbing, so the learner's ~40 lines don't grow, which keeps within
  the "keep the lab small" rule.

  One thing I'd keep separate: the stub numbers. Their order should still match
  the order learners write them in, so number within the concept groups rather
  than renumbering by concept.

  Should I log this as a dogfood finding in this form?

  between Environment and Agent with no concept of its own. So this UI change

❯ running stub 5 - I was initially a little unclear if I should be returning

  Ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Your confusion makes sense. The docs section linked from stub 5 ("Answer tool
  calls that pause the session") covers both kinds of pause in one sentence:
  "send a user.custom_tool_result or a user.tool_confirmation event." The
  deciding rule is there, but you have to piece it together:

  ┌─────────────────────────┬──────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │ The blocking event is…  │     Because…     │      You reply with…      │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │                         │ it's your tool,  │                           │
  │ agent.custom_tool_use   │ so it runs in    │ user.custom_tool_result   │
  │                         │ your process     │                           │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ agent.tool_use /        │ a permission     │ user.tool_confirmation    │
  │ agent.mcp_tool_use      │ policy asked for │ (allow/deny)              │
  │                         │  approval        │                           │
  └─────────────────────────┴──────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  The lab sets no permission policy, so only the first row ever happens. And the
  learner never builds either event by hand, because answer_custom_tool() sends
  the user.custom_tool_result for them. The ★ challenge is when to call it, not
  what to send. So the question you got stuck on was a distraction from the
  real puzzle.

  What I'd suggest for the notes:

  1. agent.py stub 5: add one line near the existing questions:
     "answer_custom_tool() sends the user.custom_tool_result for you.
     user.tool_confirmation is for permission policies, which this lab doesn't
     use (see the stretch in the README)." It removes the distraction without
     giving away the when, and it points ahead to where confirmations do matter.
  2. UI stall banner (static/index.html:256): it already says the agent "is
     waiting for you to send the result back." Add the blocking event's type and
     the expected reply, e.g. "Blocked on agent.custom_tool_use → expects
     user.custom_tool_result." It's one line, and it teaches the rule above by
     example at the moment they need it.
  3. session/likely-questions.md: add this as a Q&A, with the table above as the
     answer, so it feeds the instructor guide.
  4. Optional: tie it to the permission-policies stretch in the README. A
     learner who adds always_ask sees the second row happen for real.

  I'd skip any bigger change. Telling custom tools apart from confirmations is
  worth learning, and items 1 and 2 make it clear without turning stub 5 into
  fill-in-the-blanks.

  Should I log this?

⏺ Yes, delete that block. answer_custom_tool already sends that event, so you'd
  be answering the same tool call twice. Your copy also has three bugs that come
  from not knowing what the helper does:
  - event.send: it should be events.send.
  - Hard-coded id: "sevt_017x…" is one specific event's id, so it only works for
    that one run. The helper uses event.id.
  - result = ...: the helper returns None, so result is always empty.

  I agree the docstring should show how it works. The helper is about eight
  lines, and those lines are a Managed Agents concept: the reply event's shape,
  and the fact that custom_tool_use_id comes from the paused event's id. Showing
  it doesn't give away the ★, which is about when to call it. Proposed text for
  stub 5:

  answer_custom_tool(session_id, event) does the "you run it" half for you.
  Pass it the agent.custom_tool_use event. It returns nothing. Inside, it does:

      text = <run record_verdict locally with event.input>   # saves 
  .verdicts/<session>.json
      client.beta.sessions.events.send(session_id, events=[{
          "type": "user.custom_tool_result",
          "custom_tool_use_id": event.id,     # ← links the reply to the paused 
  call
          "content": [{"type": "text", "text": text}],
      }])

  So you never build this event yourself. (user.tool_confirmation is for
  permission policies, which this lab doesn't use.) Your job: work out WHEN to 
  call it.

  gives a clear "done" signal before moving on.

  I'd use a fixed three-part footer: the command, what passing looks like, and
  what changes in the app. I'd write it from check.py's own messages so they
  match what the learner sees:

  ✅ Check:  python check.py --step 1
     Passes when: env_… id is printed, a second call reuses it, pip-audit is 
  pre-installed.
     In the app:  the stub turns green and environment_id appears below the 
  rail.

  And for stub 5, where a run takes time and money:

  ✅ Check:  python check.py --step 5   (one full vet: ~1 min, ~5¢)
     Passes when: you answered the custom tool call, a verdict is saved locally,
                  and the turn ends with end_turn (not requires_action).
     In the app:  click Vet; the timeline ends on an end_turn badge and the
                  Verdict panel fills in.

  Things to watch:
  - Keep it in sync with check.py. This is a third place that describes each
    step, after the README and check.py. If you do the file/agent reorder, the
    step numbers in these footers change too. It's worth a line in root
    AGENTS.md rule 4.
  - Stub 6 needs a warning. --step 6 deliberately crashes a vet midway and waits
    for it to pause. Without a heads-up, that looks like something broke.
  - The README checkpoints can then shrink to pointing at the footers, so
    there's one source rather than three.

  It also helps with the earlier "UI looks empty after stubs 1–3" finding,
  because each footer tells the learner what should have changed on screen.

  Should I log it?

❯ the back and forth between the server terminal and claude code helping        
  session and agents.py and ui does feel a LITTLE much, is there a simpler way  
  to do this? maybe not?                                                        

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Partly. Some of the switching is built into the lab, but some of it is
  avoidable. Here's what a learner juggles right now:
  - Editor: agent.py.
  - Server terminal: uvicorn, which already uses --reload, so at least no manual
    restarts.
  - A second terminal: for check.py.
  - Browser: the UI.
  - Claude Code: for help.

  The editor and the UI are core. Watching the stream pause at requires_action
  and then resume is the payoff of stubs 5–7. The rest can shrink.

  1. Keep the server out of stubs 1–4. Those stubs have almost nothing to show
  in the UI (your "it only shows stubs" finding). Tell learners to use only
  check.py for stubs 1–4, and to start the server at stub 5 when there's
  something to watch. That's one fewer surface for the first ~12 minutes, and
  the README's setup section gets shorter. This is the cheapest change and I'd
  do it first.

  2. Recommend one window. In the README setup, suggest VS Code with:
  - the server in an integrated terminal,
  - Claude Code in the side panel (extension) or a split terminal,
  - the UI in a browser tab next to it.

  It's the same tools, but nothing hides behind anything else. It's a
  documentation change only.

  3. Let the UI run the checks. A "Check" button on each stub would run check.py
  --step N and stream the ✓/✗ lines into the UI. That removes the second
  terminal entirely, and it fits the concept-grouping UI finding. It costs more,
  though: a new server endpoint plus some UI work. And the step 6 crash
  simulation runs a little strangely from inside the server. I'd hold it until
  you've done 1 and 2 and see whether the switching still feels like too much.

  My recommendation is 1 and 2 now, and 3 only if a later run still feels busy.
  Want me to log it?

✻ Brewed for 17s · done 12:53 AM

❯ I actually wasn't using check.py at all, I was using claude code and the !    
  command to run check.py. I want learners to walk through this with claude     
  code. don't we think having the ui show the elements kicking in is nice?      

⏺ You're right on both counts. I was picturing a terminal-first learner, and the
  lab is meant to be worked through with Claude Code.

  The UI showing each element kick in is worth keeping. Drop my suggestion to
  keep the server out of stubs 1–4. Seeing things appear is a better fix for the
  empty early UI, and the concept-grouping finding already does that:
  Environment, Agent and Session light up as each stub lands, and clicking one
  shows what the server holds.

  With Claude Code as the hub, the simplest fix is to let it run the server too.
  Then the separate server terminal goes away:
  - Setup: lab/AGENTS.md tells Claude Code to start uvicorn server:app --reload
    in the background during setup and give the learner the URL.
  - Debugging: when something fails, Claude Code reads the server's output
    itself. The learner no longer copies tracebacks between windows, and Claude
    has the real error rather than a paraphrase.
  - Checks: ! python check.py --step N stays the way to check a stub, which is
    what you did naturally. The README and the new "✅ Check" docstring footers
    should show the ! form, so it's clearly the expected loop rather than
    something you discovered.

  That leaves three surfaces, each with a clear job:

  ┌───────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │      Surface      │                       Job                       │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Editor (agent.py) │ write the stub                                  │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Claude Code       │ help, run checks, run the server, read its logs │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Browser           │ watch it come alive                             │
  └───────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  If learners use VS Code with the Claude Code extension, the first two sit in
  one window.

  One risk: the server running in the background makes it easier to miss that
  --reload restarted after an edit, or that it crashed. lab/AGENTS.md should
  tell Claude Code to check the server log whenever the UI looks stale.

  Want me to log this, replacing the "simplify surfaces" idea?

✻ Sautéed for 4s · done 1:45 AM

❯ great, based on the updated dogfooding notes and the transcripts how can we   
  make this lab better and smoother?                                            

⏺ Your notes have about 25 separate findings, but most of them come from four
  underlying problems. Fixing those makes the lab smoother; working through the
  list one item at a time would mostly patch symptoms.

  The pattern

  Most of the time you lost went to Python and SDK mechanics, not Managed Agents
  concepts: lambdas, .id, keyword vs positional arguments, page objects vs
  lists, .version vs .version(), and what answer_custom_tool does. Every minute
  spent there is a minute not spent on the "aha" moments the lab is built for,
  like the session parking at requires_action or reattaching from history.

  One rule fixes most of it: give learners the plumbing; make them work out the 
  concepts. Show the shape of each call, so the learner spends their thinking on
  the values and the timing, which are the concepts. I'd put that sentence in
  DESIGN_NOTE.md, because it explains every change below.

  Four themes, in priority order

  1. Show call shapes and helper behaviour in agent.py (biggest win, cheapest;
  all docstrings)
  - Skeletons for stubs 1–4: the plumbing visible, the concept values blanked
    out.
  - Show each SDK call the way you type it, e.g. sessions.create(agent=..., 
    environment_id=...), instead of describing it in prose.
  - Before stub 5, show the eight-line body of answer_custom_tool, and add a
    line on custom tool results vs tool confirmations.
  - In stub 7: a skeleton of the overall structure, the "Reconnect without
    missing events" docs link, and a note that list returns a page, so wrap it
    in list(...).
  - Add a "What you're given" block at the top, so nobody has to open
    provided.py.
  - Accuracy fixes: stub 8 still needs "type": "cloud", stub 9 doesn't create
    sessions, .version is an attribute, and keep the turn_over check in stub 5.

  2. Make it obvious when a stub is done
  - A "✅ Check" footer on every stub, using the ! form.
  - Point stub 7 at --step 7.
  - check.py --step 6 should print progress while it waits, time out with a
    hint, and not look frozen inside Claude Code's 120-second limit for !
    commands.
  - The stall banner should name the blocking event type and the reply it
    expects.
  - A cleanup step at the very end of the README and of agent.py.

  3. Make Claude Code the hub and tighten its rules (lab/AGENTS.md)
  - It starts the server in the background and reads the server log itself.
  - It explains helpers by what they take and do, never by pointing at
    provided.py line numbers.
  - Verify before claiming. Of the other session's three slips, two were
    unverified claims (the missing "type", and calling a fix done before it
    was). The rule: read the SDK types before asserting what a field needs, and
    rerun the check before saying something works. It should also look at a file
    before suggesting shell edits to it (the .env newline). This matters more
    now that Claude Code is the learner's main guide.

  4. Rework the structure (bigger; do it after 1–3)
  - Reorder the stubs: environment → agent → file → session.
  - Then group the UI by the four concepts, and let learners click a resource to
    see what the server actually holds.

  Suggested sequence

  1. One pass on themes 1–3: agent.py + solutions/agent.py, lab/AGENTS.md,
     README, check.py step 6 progress, stall banner. It's mostly text, and
     check.py --solution confirms nothing broke.
  2. Dogfood again, with timings this time. Use a fresh learner if possible, or
     a Claude subagent playing the learner, as in process/learner-dry-run.md.
     This also settles the open stub 7 question: whether a skeleton is enough,
     or the stub is too hard for its slot.
  3. Theme 4, only if run 2 still feels disjointed. The reorder and UI work
     touch server.py, check.py, static/, the README and both agent.py files.
     It's worth doing, but it shouldn't be bundled with the docstring pass, so
     you can tell which change helped.

  One thing to watch: if every stub gets more scaffolding, the lab drifts toward
  fill-in-the-blanks. The test for each addition is whether it removes friction
  with Python and the SDK, or gives away a Managed Agents concept. Only the
  first kind should go in. By that test, the stub 7 docs link is fine, as you
  decided, and blanking out the networking values in stub 1 is right.

  If you want to start, I'd write step 1 up as a plan in process/ before
  changing any code.

› Message from learner-dryrun-2
  ## Lab dogfood report (learner run, handle dev-subagent-2)

  I never opened `lab/solutions/`, `process/` or `provided.py`. The only files I
  changed are `lab/agent.py` and `lab/.env`. **There are no "✅ Check" footers
  in this commit's `lab/agent.py`** (worktree at fb0affc, the same as main
  HEAD). So I used the README's `check.py --step N` commands, and I couldn't
  judge footer durations or "passes when" text. If the footers exist, they're
  uncommitted in the main checkout.

  ### Checkpoint results
  | Step | Result | Attempts | Wall time |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | 0 | pass | 1 | 1 s |
  | 1–4 | pass | 1 each | about 5 s total |
  | 5 | as-is run hung (see below); pass after the fix | 2 | hung for 5 min 53 s
  before I killed it; the passing run took 47 s |
  | 7 (= 6) | pass | 1 | 55 s (parked after 47 s) |
  | 8 | pass | 1 | 2 s |
  | 9 | pass | 1 | 3 s |

  I also ran one extra `--step 5` after lockdown to test the README's step-8
  claims. The evidence said `curl: (6) Could not resolve host`, the verdict was
  still BLOCK (risk 8), and pip-audit still ran.

  **Time:** about 11 minutes for me in total, about 6 of them lost to the hang.
  For a human at this level I'd estimate 0–8 min setup, 10–15 min for stubs 1–4,
  10–15 for stub 5, 10–15 for stubs 6–7 and 10 for the stretch stubs. That fits
  the 60 minutes, provided nobody hits the hang below.

  ### Confusing or inaccurate spots
  1. **`check.py --step 5` hangs forever on unmodified code.**
     - README:97–98 tells you to run it.
     - `turn_over()` doesn't fire on `requires_action`, so the stream goes
  silent. `drain()`'s timeout (check.py:53) is only tested when an event
  arrives, so it never fires.
     - The helpful `Fail("…requires_action — it's waiting on your app")` at
  check.py:115 is therefore unreachable.
     - A CLI-only learner sees a frozen terminal, not a hint. This matters most
  for anyone without the UI.
  2. **The docs' "List past events" Python sample iterates `events.data` (first
  page only).** A learner who copies it into `resume()` may miss the last
  `status_idle`. The stub-7 docstring at agent.py:155 should say "iterate the
  page, which auto-paginates".
  3. **README:136 says "No check can reliably catch a wrong ordering", but
  README:111/142 and agent.py:112 already hand over the ordering.** The stub-5
  comment reads "Open the stream BEFORE sending, so no early events are missed",
  and the README nudge says the docs give "the order in three numbered steps".
  4. **README:150 says `python check.py` takes "≈2-3 min".** Steps 5 + 6 alone
  took about 100 s for me, so that's plausible, but README:128 calls the gate
  `--step 6` while I ran `--step 7`. They are the same check, and that isn't
  stated anywhere.
  5. **The README:107 heads-up says a reload "will find" the old parked
  session.** It won't: `find_open_session` returns the *newest* session, so my
  first parked session (sesn_01Es3…) was never resumed. `cleanup.py` removed it.
  6. **`turn_over()` semantics aren't documented in agent.py.** I inferred "not
  on `requires_action`" only from the hang. A one-line note would help.
  7. **Step 9 runs before a step-8 re-vet, so the lockdown vet also ran on v2.**
  The verdict cited "Policy v2", which muddies the step-8 observation.

  **Not verified (UI):** the grey rail, the ✓ markers, resource IDs under the

  ### How much reasoning was left
  - **Stub 5:** almost fully pasteable. The docs' "Return a custom tool result"
  sample is the answer, minus swapping in `answer_custom_tool`. The only real
  thought was whether the `requires_action` idle would end the loop after
  answering.
  - **Stub 7:** mostly pasteable. Of the four questions:

  troubleshooting table (README:201, 203).

  agent.py:112 cues it strongly.

  the docstring, so I didn't have to work them out.

  ### provided.py
  I never needed it. The only gap was the `turn_over` semantics above.
  `BUDGET_CENTS` turned out to already be in the right type (the docs require a
  string).

  ### Calibration
  - **Friction a human would hit that I didn't:**
    - generator/`yield` inside a `with` block
    - the files-upload tuple
    - `state.sync` taking a lambda
    - remembering `.id`
    - reading 1,800 lines of the events doc to find the right section
  - **Friction I hit that a human might not:** buffered stdout in a background
  run hid the progress lines. A human in a terminal would at least see "running
  a full vet…" before the freeze.

  ### Top 5 suggested edits
  1. Make `drain()` time out without needing an event (use a stream read
  timeout, or poll `sessions.retrieve` status), so the check.py:115 hint
  actually prints.
  2. Add a one-liner to stub 5 or the README: "`turn_over()` is true for
  `end_turn` and terminal states, not for `requires_action`."
  3. Pull README:201/203 out of the troubleshooting table, or trim the stub-5
  comment, so stub 7's Q2/Q4 need thought. Or accept that it's guided and drop
  the ★.
  4. Warn in stub 7 about the docs sample's `.data` first-page pitfall.
  5. Fix the README:107 claim, or say "the newest open session wins". Also state
  that `--step 6` and `--step 7` run the same check, and suggest re-vetting
  step 8 before doing step 9.

@ slides-critic❯ Slides design critique report
  Design critique of session/slides.html: it's intentional and teachable, but
  has projector-legibility and color-meaning problems. No files were edited.

  Verdict. This reads as a deliberate talk deck, not a template. There's one
  spine (who runs the loop), one metaphor carried through, sketch diagrams that
  do real explaining, and slide-level rules ("idle ≠ done"). Two things look
  generic. The palette is the cream #f4f1ea + terracotta default the
  frontend-design skill flags as the AI look. And the bodies alternate between
  uppercase eyebrows and card grids (slides 4 and 6). Projected, the concept
  slides (1, 3, 5) will work. The slides that matter most for the lab (7, 8, 10)
  rely on 12–15px SVG text and a hand font that won't exist on a Windows
  projector PC.

  Per slide
  - 1 Title: the arrow cuts through the "events in" label. The footer and the
    floating #hint say the same thing.
  - 2 Loop: good. 16px code is borderline. The circle has almost no direction
    cue.
  - 3 Who runs what: the strongest slide. It's clear and legible, and the legend
    teaches orange = you, blue = Anthropic.
  - 4 When each wins: 12 bullets, each wrapping to 2 lines, with decorative
    icons. Too much text for 75 s.
  - 5 Kitchen: lovely. The 16px sublabels are small and the bottom 20% is empty.
  - 6 Metaphor breaks: six 25-word cards. Good teaching, too dense for 75 s.
  - 7 Anatomy of a turn (the most important slide): event names are 12–13px
    mono. That's about 14px at 1440 and under 12px at 1280×720, so it's
    unreadable past row 3. The top third is empty. The #rough filter makes the
    mono text wobble. Amber-on-amber-bg contrast is about 3.2:1, which fails at
    that size. user.message (accent red) and custom_tool_use (orange) are hard
    to tell apart on a washed-out projector or for colour-blind viewers.
    Everything appears at once, with no build.
  - 8 Two zones: "pip install vendor-pkg" overflows its box. The arrow labels
    collide with the box borders. Conceptually, the arrows run sandbox ↔ your
    process, but the custom-tool call comes from the agent/session, not the
    container. Slide 10 even says "Claude + event log on Anthropic's side."
    "Some run in you" reads oddly.
  - 9 Versioned object: "prompt · tools · model" overflows the v1 box. The web
    app / cron / Slack column floats with no connector to an agent ID. It
    overlaps slide 6 card 1 and the "v3" on slide 5.
  - 10 Cloud vs self-hosted: the diagram is scaled down (12–15px text) while the
    bottom 35% of the slide is empty. The "✗ example.com" line doesn't read as
    blocked. "Beyond today" is a run-on list.
  - 11 See it, build it: the left card overruns the bottom padding and nearly
    touches the slide edge. The lab screenshot is an unreadable dark thumbnail
    at about 550px.

  System level
  - Color meaning drifts. Orange means "you" (3, 8, 10) but also Agent SDK (4),
    Session (5) and Anthropic-run sessions (9). Purple means Messages API (4)
    but also Agent (5, 9). Blue also means Environment (5). This undoes the key
    slide 3 teaches.
  - Type: the bold, tightly tracked system sans headings are fine. The hand font
    stack is Chalkboard SE (macOS only), then Segoe Print or Comic Sans on
    Windows. The deck is offline-only, so it needs an embedded font.
  - Filter: #rough applies to whole SVGs, text included. Apply it to shapes
    only.
  - Dark mode works (checked). There's no motion, which suits most slides. Slide
    7 needs a 3-step build.
  - Notes: N overlays the notes on the same screen. With a mirrored projector,
    the room sees them. There's no presenter view. The counter floats into the
    slide when notes are open.

  Pacing. 11 slides in 12 minutes (about 65 s each) is fine on count. Density is
  the problem. Cut slide 9 and fold its point into a card on slide 6. Cut slide
  6 to the 3–4 breaks the lab hits (pinning, requires_action, sealed network,
  replay). Drop "Beyond today" from slide 10 into the notes. That leaves 10
  slides with breathing room for slide 7.

  Top 7 fixes, ranked
  1. Make slide 7 readable. Drop span.model_request_start and status_running.
     Use 18–20px labels and the empty top third. Remove #rough from the text.
     Build it in 3 steps: sandbox work, pause, resume.
  2. Lock one color meaning: orange = your process, blue = Anthropic-operated,
     purple = agent definition. Recolor slide 4's cards with neutral or
     product-specific marks, slide 5's ticket, and slide 9's sessions.
  3. Embed one hand font as a base64 woff2 (for example Kalam or Excalifont,
     both OFL) so projector PCs don't fall back to Comic Sans. Limit #rough to
     .sk shapes.
  4. Fix slide 8. Route both arrows from a "session / Claude" node on
     Anthropic's side to your process, not from the sandbox box. Widen the pip
     box and move the arrow labels clear of the borders.
  5. Cut text on slides 4 and 6: at most 2–3 short bullets per card on 4, 3–4
     cards on 6. Merge slide 9 into 6.
  6. Fix slide 11. Clamp the left card's height (the heatmap's max-height) and
     replace the lab thumbnail with a cropped detail (one sandbox card, one
     local card, the BLOCK verdict).
  7. Projector hygiene. Darken amber and orange text tokens to at least 4.5:1 on
     their backgrounds, remove the duplicate #hint, and add a presenter window
     (?presenter opens notes in a popup) or document "extend the display, don't
     mirror" in the instructor guide.

  Couldn't verify: a real projector or washout, Windows/Linux font fallback
  (inferred from the font stack), colour-blind rendering (contrast ratios are
  hand-calculated), and the reading distance in an actual room.

⏺ The critique holds up. I checked its two biggest claims against the
  screenshots, and both are right:
  - Slide 7: at 1280×720 the event labels are tiny, and the top third of the
    slide is empty.
  - Slide 8: the custom-tool arrows run from the sandbox box to your process.

  Here's my view, in roughly the order I'd act on it.

  Strongly agree:
  1. Slide 8 is teaching the wrong picture, and I'd make it #1, above 
     legibility. record_verdict is called by the agent through the session, not
     by the sandbox container. Your own slide 10 says "Claude + event log on
     Anthropic's side." The lab's whole "who runs what" lesson depends on this.
     The slide draws Anthropic's side as one blue sandbox, so learners would
     come away thinking the container calls your code. The fix is to split
     Anthropic's side into two parts, the agent/session and the sandbox, and
     route the custom-tool arrows from the agent/session.
  2. Slide 7 legibility and a three-step build. It's the slide the lab's first ★
     stub depends on, and right now it's unreadable from the back of a room.
     I'd also drop span.model_request_start and status_running, as it suggests.
     Neither matters for the lesson.
  3. One fixed meaning per colour. Slide 3 teaches orange = you, blue =
     Anthropic, and the lab UI uses the same colours (blue sandbox, orange
     local), so consistency here pays off in the lab as well. Recolouring slides
     4, 5 and 9 is a cheap fix.
  4. Embed the hand font. The deck is offline-only, and on a Windows projector
     PC it falls back to Comic Sans, which would undercut the whole deck.
     Limiting the rough-edged sketch filter (#rough) to the drawn shapes also
     fixes the wobbly code text.

  Agree, with changes:
  - Trim slides 4 and 6, but keep the right items. On slide 6, keep the breaks
    the lab actually hits: pinning, requires_action, sealed network, permanent
    record. On slide 4, keep the line that Managed Agents isn't ZDR or HIPAA
    eligible, even if other bullets go. Customers ask about it, and it's the
    kind of accuracy point a reviewer would check.
  - Don't cut slide 9. It suggests folding versioning into slide 6. But "the
    agent is versioned config" is one of the deck's two central ideas
    (DESIGN_NOTE.md row 1) and is what stretch 9 exercises. Simplify the slide
    instead: fix the box that overflows and connect the web app / cron / Slack
    column to the agent ID.
  - Move slide 10's "Beyond today" list into the speaker notes. Agreed.

  Skip or defer:
  - The cream-and-terracotta palette as the "AI look." That's a matter of taste.
    The deck already has a clear point of view, and a palette overhaul is a lot
    of work for little gain in understanding.
  - A presenter view. For now, one line in the instructor guide ("extend the
    display, don't mirror it") solves the speaker-notes problem.
  - Slide 11's thumbnail and the duplicate hint text. Real but minor. Do them if
    you're already editing those slides.

  Suggested order: slide 8 correctness → slide 7 → colour meanings → font and
  filter → trims to 4 and 6 → the rest. The first four are where an audience
  would actually misunderstand something or be unable to read it.

⏺ Teammate @slides-critic finished
  I sent the slide critique to team-lead. I didn't edit anything in the deck.

  Verdict: the deck looks deliberate, not templated. One idea (who runs the
  loop) and one restaurant-kitchen metaphor carry all the way through, and the
  hand-drawn diagrams do real teaching. Two things look generic: the cream and
  terracotta colours, and the card-grid layout on slides 4 and 6. The concept
  slides (1, 3, 5) will work on a projector. The slides the lab leans on (7, 8,
  10) have 12–15px diagram text that won't read from the back. They also use a
  handwriting font that only exists on Macs.

  Top fixes, ranked:
  1. Slide 7 (event timeline): this matters most and is the least readable.
     Event names render at about 12px. Cut it to 6 events, use 18–20px labels
     and fill the empty top third. Take the wobble effect off the text and
     reveal it in 3 steps.
  2. Colours change meaning between slides. Orange means "you run it" on slides
     3, 8 and 10, but it also marks Agent SDK, Session and Anthropic-run
     sessions elsewhere. That undoes the key slide 3 teaches. Pick one meaning
     per colour and recolour the rest.
  3. Fonts: embed one free handwriting font in the file. Otherwise a Windows
     projector PC falls back to Comic Sans. Apply the wobble effect to shapes
     only, not text.
  4. Slide 8 diagram is wrong. Its arrows run between the sandbox and your
     process. The custom-tool call actually comes from the session on
     Anthropic's side, as slide 10 says. Some text there also overflows its box
     or overlaps arrows.
  5. Too much text: slide 4 has 12 two-line bullets and slide 6 has six 25-word
     cards. Fold slide 9 into slide 6, since it repeats the versioning point.
     That leaves about 10 slides for 12 minutes.
  6. Slide 11: the left card runs past the bottom margin, and the lab screenshot
     is too small to read. Swap in a cropped detail of the app.
  7. Projector:
     - Darken the amber and orange text; amber fails contrast at about 3.2:1.
     - Remove the duplicate keyboard-hint line.
     - The speaker notes overlay the slide itself, so a mirrored projector shows
       them to the room. Add a presenter window, or tell the instructor to
       extend the display rather than mirror it.

  Not checked: a real projector, how Windows or Linux actually renders the fonts
  (I inferred it from the font list), colour-blind rendering, and reading
  distance in a real room. Contrast ratios are hand-calculated.

  The local file:// URL was blocked in the browser, so I served the deck from a
  temporary local server, which is now stopped.

  - 07-1280-dark.png and 11-1280-dark.png: dark mode at 1280

⏺ The slides are done, in six commits on dogfood-pass-1. The learner dry run is
  still going; I'll tell you when it finishes.

  What changed in the deck:
  - 11 → 9 slides. I cut "You've written this loop", as you suggested, and
    "Where the metaphor breaks". "When each one wins" stays, down to three short
    bullets per card. None of the cut slides' speaker notes were dropped:
    - the loop framing and the code-execution question went to "Who runs what"
    - the 30-day sandbox state went to the kitchen slide
    - interrupt, replay and idle cost went to "Anatomy of one turn"

    Slide timings are rebalanced to 12:00, with about 2 minutes for the turn
    anatomy.
  - Two-zones diagram corrected. Anthropic's side is now drawn as the session
    plus its sandbox. agent.custom_tool_use runs from the session to your
    process, and the sandbox only talks to the session.
  - Turn anatomy:
    - Labels go from 12px to 18px, with two noise events removed.
    - The empty top third is used.
    - It builds in three clicks: sandbox work, then the pause, then the resume.
      Going back reverses the build, and re-entering the slide starts it from
      the beginning.
  - One colour meaning throughout: orange = your process, blue = Anthropic
    operates, purple = definitions you write once (agent, environment).
  - Contrast:
    - Light-mode text colours now all compute to at least 4.5:1 against their
      own backgrounds.
    - Dark mode already passed.
    - No diagram text is smaller than 14.5px.
  - Font: Kalam (free, OFL-licensed) is embedded, about 60KB, so a Windows
    projector no longer falls back to Comic Sans. I confirmed in the browser
    that it loads and is the font in use. The rough sketch effect now applies
    only to shapes, so text stays crisp.
  - Smaller fixes:
    - all three apps on the versioned-agent slide now connect to one agent ID
    - the blocked host on the cloud slide reads as blocked
    - "Beyond today" moved to the speaker notes
    - the title slide's label no longer sits under its arrow, and the duplicate
      keyboard hint is gone
    - the notebook card fits its slide

  I checked every changed slide by screenshot at 1280×720, with the turn anatomy
  and cloud slides in dark mode as well.

  Recorded: a new DESIGN_NOTE.md row (15) explains the cuts and what the
  critique caught. The empty Session section in process/dogfood-notes.md now has
  the outcomes and what's still open.

⏺ The docs say it exactly: "Claude Managed Agents is built around four
  concepts." I found about eight places in the repo that say "objects":
  - the kitchen slide's kicker
  - the lab README's table header
  - lab/AGENTS.md's hint ladder
  - the notebook's first table
  - three lines in DESIGN_NOTE.md

  I'll change all of them. The archived planning logs in process/ I'd leave
  alone, since they're a record of what was said at the time. One judgment call:
  "The agent is a versioned API object" is about the agent being an API
  resource, not about the four concepts. I'd still change it to "versioned API
  resource", to match the docs and avoid any echo of "object".

  On merging slides 5 and 6: yes, they overlap. Both draw the custom-tool round
  trip and both teach "blue = sandbox, orange = you". But each has something the
  other lacks:
  - Slide 5 (anatomy) is about when: the order of events, and the pause at
    requires_action. It maps directly onto the lab's timeline UI.
  - Slide 6 (two zones) is about where: who runs which tool, plus the rule of
    thumb about secrets.

  Merging them into one diagram with three swim lanes would keep both views and
  remove the duplicate round trip:

   your process  ●user.message ─────────────────────────────────────── 
  ●custom_tool_result ──────────────
                      ↓                                      ↑ (orange)       ↓
   session       ─────●──────●──────────●──── ●custom_tool_use ── ⏸ 
  requires_action ──●agent.message ──● end_turn
                           ↓ tool_use  ↑ tool_result
   sandbox       ──── [ built-in toolset ] 
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
                 time →

  - Lanes: your process (orange), session/Claude (blue), sandbox (lighter blue,
    labelled just "built-in toolset" as you suggested).
  - Time runs left to right, like the lab UI.
  - Every event becomes an arrow between lanes, so you can read where from its
    lane and when from its position.
  - The same 3-step build: sandbox work, then the pause, then the answer and
    end_turn.
  - The rule of thumb (disposable box → sandbox; your systems or secrets →
    custom tool) goes in the speaker notes, or as a footer on step 3.

  What it gains: 8 slides, roughly another 75 seconds back, and one diagram that
  answers both "where" and "when". It also fixes an ordering problem in the
  current deck: slide 5 shows custom_tool_use before slide 6 has explained what
  a custom tool is.

  What it costs: it's the densest diagram in the deck, so it relies on the
  build, and the event labels have to stay at 18px across a three-lane layout.
  It's also the biggest redraw so far. I'd sketch it, screenshot all three steps
  at 1280×720, and if it's cramped, fall back to keeping both slides but
  swapping their order: zones first, anatomy second.

  The rest of your list, for the same batch:
  - Step 9: a docstring note that the check passes only once per agent, since a
    rerun with the same prompt creates no new version.
  - The red ✗ on the expected stall: turn it into a neutral, non-red line that
    reads as expected, e.g. "⏸ stalled at requires_action — that's the stub-5
    puzzle, not a bug".
  - Step label: the full run will label the reattach check [6–7], the same as
    --step 6 and --step 7.
  - Stub 8: a note that the docs describe a 403 for blocked hosts, but in this
    lab you'll see "Could not resolve host", because the DNS lookup fails first.
  - Unchanged, as you asked: the timing claims and the smaller items.

  Should I go ahead with the merged swim-lane slide, falling back to the swap if
  it's cramped, plus all of the above, in one pass?

          thropic runs the loop." Kill the app, and the session finishes

          ack as an event. A learner who can explain the amber banner has

  Merged slide (the deck is now 8 slides): "Who does what, and when" is a
  swim-lane diagram. The rows are your process, the session (Claude runs the
  loop) and the sandbox, which is labelled just "built-in toolset", and time
  runs left to right. It builds one stage per click:

  ┌───────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Click │                           What appears                           │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1     │ You send user.message                                            │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 2     │ The session calls the sandbox (tool_use ↓, tool_result ↑)        │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 3     │ The session asks your process (custom_tool_use, "record_verdict  │
  │       │ runs here"), then pauses at idle · requires_action               │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 4     │ You answer (custom_tool_result), then agent.message, end_turn,   │
  │       │ and the rule "idle ≠ done"                                       │
  └───────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
